What she expected to be a graceful easing into more time for hiking, cultural festivals, and family gatherings instead left her feeling unmoored, with stretches of overcommitting to new volunteer and sustainability projects followed by periods where she withdrew from her book club and left household tasks unfinished. Her family noticed that in the months after reducing her workload, her mood and drive seemed to swing more sharply than the stress she described, and she became increasingly preoccupied with whether she had \"wasted\" her life and career despite others’ reassurance. When she confided to her daughter during a Smoky Mountains planning session that she had been dwelling on death and questioning whether her family would be better off without her, her daughter brought thoughts race so fast—from retirement, to climate change, to my grandkids’ school, to how to redo the HOA rules—that I can’t finish one idea before another one crashes in, and it’s hard to shut my brain off to sleep. \n- S040 (Increased_Goal_Activity): I went on a tear signing up for extra shifts on the sustainability committee, promising to help at church, and even offering to manage a neighbor’s rental paperwork, all at once, even though I was already overwhelmed and couldn’t keep up. \n- S041 (Painful_Consequence_Behavior): In one of those 'up' periods I almost put a big chunk of our savings into a fancy eco‑housing project after reading about it online, telling myself I knew better than the financial guy, and my daughter had to talk me down when I mentioned it. \n▸ Additional neighbor’s rental paperwork, all at once, even though I was already overwhelmed and couldn’t keep up. \n- S041 (Painful_Consequence_Behavior): In one of those 'up' periods I almost put a big chunk of What she expected to be a graceful easing into more time for hiking, cultural festivals, and family gatherings instead left her feeling unmoored, with stretches of overcommitting to new volunteer and sustainability projects followed by periods where she withdrew from her book club and left household tasks unfinished. Her family noticed that in the months after reducing her workload, her mood and drive seemed to swing more sharply than the stress she described, and she became increasingly preoccupied with whether she had \"wasted\" her life and career despite others’ reassurance. When she confided to her daughter during a Smoky Mountains planning session that she had been dwelling on death and questioning whether her family would be better off without her, her daughter brought her to an urgent primary care visit, which led to an expedited psychiatric evaluation.\n- Chief Complaint: “I’ve been thinking about death a lot and wondering if my family would be better off without me.”\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S025 (Loss_of_Interest): Lately even the things I was looking forward to doing in retirement, like my hikes and the neighborhood festival committee, go all day on a cup of coffee and then just push my dinner around the plate, and my clothes are starting to feel loose. \n- S028 (Psychomotor_Changes): My husband keeps asking why I’m moving so slowly, but it’s like my body is heavy as concrete—getting up from the chair, walking up the stairs, even reaching for the phone feels like it’s in slow motion. \n- S030 (Worthlessness_Guilt): I keep life. \n- S031 (Diminished_Thinking): My mind feels foggy—I'll read the same page in my book club novel three times and still not take it in, and I stand in the kitchen unable to decide something simple like what to make for breakfast. \n- S032 (Death_Suicide): Every night when I lie down I find myself thinking that it wouldn’t be so bad if I just didn’t wake up, and I’ve even caught myself picturing ways I could make that happen so my husband and kids wouldn’t have to deal with me dragging them down anymore. \n- S033 (Elevated_Irritable_Mood): Then there was this stretch a few weeks back where, out of nowhere, I felt wired and touchy at the same time—snapping at my husband over tiny things but also feeling oddly upbeat and talking a mile a minute about all these new projects I could take on. \n- S034 (Increased_Activity): During that time I was cutting back at work, I suddenly had this burst where I was up before dawn cleaning closets, reorganizing files, and planning three different volunteer drives at once, like I couldn’t sit still. \n- S035 (Inflated_Self_Esteem): In those more wound‑up spells, I catch myself thinking, 'I’m the only one who can really fix how our whole condo community runs,' like I’m some sort of expert who sees what nobody else can, even though I know that sounds over the top. \n- S038 (Racing_Thoughts): When I get in those high gears my thoughts race so fast—from retirement, to climate change, to my grandkids’ school, to how to redo the HOA rules—that I can’t finish one idea before another one crashes in, and it’s hard to shut my brain off to sleep. \n- S040 (Increased_Goal_Activity): I went on a tear signing up for extra shifts on the sustainability committee, promising to help at church, and even offering to manage a neighbor’s rental paperwork, all at once, even though I was already overwhelmed and couldn’t keep up. \n- S041 (Painful_Consequence_Behavior): In one of those 'up' periods I almost put a big chunk of our savings into a fancy eco‑housing project after reading about it online, telling myself I knew better than the financial guy, and my daughter had to talk me down when I mentioned it. \n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- functional_impairment: Since all this started getting worse, I’ve had to back out of managing even the few properties I kept, I’ve stopped showing up to my book club and some family get‑togethers, and my husband has been picking up a lot of the cooking and bills because I just can’t keep on top of things.\n- duration: Looking back, I feel like these swings between different volunteer drives at once, like I couldn’t sit still. \n- S035 (Inflated_Self_Esteem): In those more wound‑up spells, I catch myself thinking, 'I’m the only one who can really fix how our brain off to sleep. \n- S040 (Increased_Goal_Activity): I went on a tear signing up for extra shifts on the sustainability committee, promising to help at church, and even offering to manage a neighbor’s rental paperwork, all at once, even though I was already overwhelmed and couldn’t keep up. \n- S041 (Painful_Consequence_Behavior): In one of those 'up' periods I almost put a big chunk of our savings into a fancy eco‑housing project after reading about it online, telling myself I knew better than the financial guy, and my daughter had to talk me down when I mentioned it. \n▸ Additional different volunteer drives at once, like I couldn’t sit still. \n- S035 (Inflated_Self_Esteem): In those more wound‑up spells, I catch myself thinking, 'I’m the only one who can really fix how our whole condo community runs,' like I’m some sort of expert who sees what nobody else can, even though I know that sounds over the top. \n- S038 (Racing_Thoughts): When I get in those high gears my brain off to sleep. \n- S040 (Increased_Goal_Activity): I went on a tear signing up for extra shifts on the sustainability committee, promising to help at church, and even offering to manage a neighbor’s rental paperwork, all at once, even though I was already overwhelmed and couldn’t keep up. \n- S041 (Painful_Consequence_Behavior): In one of those 'up' periods I almost put a big chunk of our savings into a fancy eco‑housing project after reading about it online, telling myself I knew better than the financial guy, and my daughter had to talk me down when I mentioned it. \n▸ Additional Throughout this entire year, I've just kept swinging back and forth between being wound-up and sinking into these dark, heavy periods.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any period of at least two months during this past year when your mood and interest went back to normal, or has the depression been present almost continuously?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't had any breaks like that. Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, I’m Dr. ____. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I’ve been thinking about death a lot and wondering if my family would be better off without me. I told my daughter about it when we were planning a trip, and she got so worried she brought me here to get help.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a similar episode of depression or feeling this way in the past, or is this the first time?\n\nPatient: These heavy feelings aren't entirely brand new, but they've been coming and going for about a year now, especially since we started talking about my retirement. It has really ramped up and gotten much worse since I actually started cutting back my hours at work.\n\nDoctor: Did these symptoms start only after the retirement/work changes, or were you feeling depressed for a longer period before that as well?\n\nPatient: It really started about a year ago when my husband and I first began seriously discussing my retirement. I wasn't feeling this way before those talks, but once we started planning to ease me out of work, these heavy moods began and then got much worse when I actually cut my hours.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or losing interest most days for at least two weeks at a time, even when the retirement/work changes are not on your mind?\n\nPatient: Yes, I've felt completely flat and lost interest in everything I used to love, like hiking and the neighborhood festival, pretty much steadily for about a year now. It feels like a heavy, hopeless cloud that just won't go away, even when I'm not thinking about work or retirement. It's there almost every single day.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of hurting yourself or making a plan to end your life?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have. Every night when I lie down, I find myself thinking it would be easier if I just didn't wake up at all. But I haven't actually done anything to prepare or taken any physical steps to go through with it. It's just a heavy, constant thought when I'm lying in bed.\n\nDoctor: Did these symptoms begin only after a specific stressful event or life change, or have they been present for at least two weeks without a clear trigger?\n\nPatient: They really started ramping up about a year ago when my husband and I began seriously discussing my retirement and I started cutting back my work hours. These dark, heavy spells and mood swings have been going on steadily for about a year now, which is much longer than just a couple of weeks.\n\nDoctor: Before the retirement changes started, did you have any depression or loss of interest lasting at least two weeks, or did these symptoms begin only after that stressor?\n\nPatient: No, I didn't experience these kinds of prolonged, dark periods before we started talking about my retirement. Those heavy feelings and the loss of interest really only began about a year ago when my husband and I first had those serious discussions. While things have ramped up significantly since I actually cut my work hours, I was doing fine before we started planning for retirement.\n\nDoctor: Did this start after a specific stressful event or loss, and since then have you had symptoms like feeling constantly on edge, nightmares, flashbacks, or avoiding reminders of it?\n\nPatient: While my mood struggles definitely began around the transition of planning my retirement and cutting back on work hours, I have not experienced nightmares, flashbacks, or any of those other symptoms. Those specific things aren't something I've been dealing with at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you been through a specific traumatic event or major stressful loss, and since then have you had symptoms like intrusive memories, nightmares, flashbacks, or avoiding reminders of it?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't gone through any traumatic events or major losses like that. While retiring has been a very stressful transition for me, I don't have nightmares, flashbacks, or those kinds of symptoms.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms been tied mainly to the retirement/work change and improved when the stress is less, or have they stayed present most days regardless of what is happening in your life?\n\nPatient: Even though it started with the retirement changes, these feelings haven't improved when the stress is low; they've stayed present almost every day. This heavy, hopeless depression has hung over me steadily for about a year now. Throughout this entire year, I've just kept swinging back and forth between being wound-up and sinking into these dark, heavy periods.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any period of at least two months during this past year when your mood and interest went back to normal, or has the depression been present almost continuously?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't had any breaks like that. This heavy, hopeless depression has been present almost continuously for about a year now, ever since we first started talking about my retirement. I haven't had any periods where I felt back to normal during this past year.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods during this past year when you felt unusually energized, needed much less sleep, talked more than usual, or felt overly confident or restless?\n\nPatient: Yes, I had a stretch a few weeks ago where I felt wired, touchy, and was talking a mile a minute. I had this sudden burst of energy where I was up before dawn reorganizing everything and felt like I was the only expert who could fix our condo community. My thoughts were racing so fast that it was incredibly hard to shut my brain off to sleep.\n\nDoctor: Have these energized periods lasted at least 4 days, and during them did you need much less sleep or do things that caused problems at work, at home, or with other people?\n\nPatient: I’m not entirely sure how many days those periods lasted or how to answer the sleep part, though I definitely had a hard time shutting my brain off. I do know that I caused some worry with my family during those times, especially when my daughter had to talk me down from a risky financial decision.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of several days or more when you felt unusually energized or irritable with much less need for sleep, more talkative, racing thoughts, or risky behavior that was clearly different from your usual self?\n\nPatient: Yes, I definitely had a stretch like that a few weeks back where I felt wired, irritable, and oddly upbeat out of nowhere, talking a mile a minute with racing thoughts. I was waking up before dawn to clean, reorganizing everything, and overcommitting to all kinds of volunteer projects. It was so unlike me that I even almost put a huge chunk of our savings into a risky housing project before my daughter talked me down.\n\nDoctor: During that energized period, did it last at least 1 week, or did it cause you to need hospitalization or seriously disrupt work, home life, or relationships?"

It's just a heavy, constant thought when I'm lying in bed.\n\nDoctor: Did these symptoms begin only after a specific stressful event or life change, or have they been present for at least two weeks without a clear trigger?\n\nPatient: They really started ramping up about a year ago when my husband and I began seriously discussing my retirement and I started cutting back my work hours. These dark, heavy spells and mood swings have been going on steadily for about a year now, which is much longer than just a couple of weeks.\n\nDoctor: Before the retirement changes started, did you have any depression or loss of interest lasting at least two weeks, or did these symptoms begin only after that stressor?\n\nPatient: No, I didn't experience these kinds of prolonged, dark periods before we started talking about my retirement. Those heavy feelings and the loss of interest really only began about a year ago when my husband and I first had those serious discussions. While things have ramped up significantly since I actually cut my work hours, I was doing fine before we started planning for retirement.\n\nDoctor: Did this start after a specific stressful event or loss, and since then have you had symptoms like feeling constantly on edge, nightmares, flashbacks, or avoiding reminders of it?\n\nPatient: While my mood struggles definitely began around the transition of planning my retirement and cutting back on work hours, I have not experienced nightmares, flashbacks, or any of those other symptoms. Those specific things aren't something I've been dealing with at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you been through a specific traumatic event or major stressful loss, and since then have you had symptoms like intrusive memories, nightmares, flashbacks, or avoiding reminders of it?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't gone through any traumatic events or major losses like that. While retiring has been a very stressful transition for me, I don't have nightmares, flashbacks, or those kinds of symptoms.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms been tied mainly to the retirement/work change and improved when the stress is less, or have they stayed present most days regardless of what is happening in your life?\n\nPatient: Even though it started with the retirement changes, these feelings haven't improved when the stress is low; they've stayed present almost every day. This heavy, hopeless depression has hung over me steadily for about a year now. Throughout this entire year, I've just kept swinging back and forth between being wound-up and sinking into these dark, heavy periods.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any period of at least two months during this past year when your mood and interest went back to normal, or has the depression been present almost continuously?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't had any breaks like that. This heavy, hopeless depression has been present almost continuously for about a year now, ever since we first started talking about my retirement. I haven't had any periods where I felt back to normal during this past year.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods during this past year when you felt unusually energized, needed much less sleep, talked more than usual, or felt overly confident or restless?\n\nPatient: Yes, I had a stretch a few weeks ago where I felt wired, touchy, and was talking a mile a minute. I had this sudden burst of energy where I was up before dawn reorganizing everything and felt like I was the only expert who could fix our condo community. My thoughts were racing so fast that it was incredibly hard to shut my brain off to sleep.\n\nDoctor: Have these energized periods lasted at least 4 days, and during them did you need much less sleep or do things that caused problems at work, at home, or with other people?\n\nPatient: I’m not entirely sure how many days those periods lasted or how to answer the sleep part, though I definitely had a hard time shutting my brain off. I do know that I caused some worry with my family during those times, especially when my daughter had to talk me down from a risky financial decision.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of several days or more when you felt unusually energized or irritable with much less need for sleep, more talkative, racing thoughts, or risky behavior that was clearly different from your usual self?\n\nPatient: Yes, I definitely had a stretch like that a few weeks back where I felt wired, irritable, and oddly upbeat out of nowhere, talking a mile a minute with racing thoughts. I was waking up before dawn to clean, reorganizing everything, and overcommitting to all kinds of volunteer projects. It was so unlike me that I even almost put a huge chunk of our savings into a risky housing project before my daughter talked me down.\n\nDoctor: During that energized period, did it last at least 1 week, or did it cause you to need hospitalization or seriously disrupt work, home life, or relationships?\n\nPatient: No, that energized period only lasted for several days—less than a week—and I was never hospitalized. While I almost made that big mistake with our savings, the severe disruption to my daily life and work has really been driven by the heavy, dark periods. It's the overall toll of these unpredictable swings that has made me back away from everything."
  }

While retiring has been a very stressful transition for me, I don't have nightmares, flashbacks, or those kinds of symptoms.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms been tied mainly to the retirement/work change and improved when the stress is less, or have they stayed present most days regardless of what is happening in your life?\n\nPatient: Even though it started with the retirement changes, these feelings haven't improved when the stress is low; they've stayed present almost every day. This heavy, hopeless depression has hung over me steadily for about a year now. Throughout this entire year, I've just kept swinging back and forth between being wound-up and sinking into these dark, heavy periods.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any period of at least two months during this past year when your mood and interest went back to normal, or has the depression been present almost continuously?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't had any breaks like that. This heavy, hopeless depression has been present almost continuously for about a year now, ever since we first started talking about my retirement. I haven't had any periods where I felt back to normal during this past year.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods during this past year when you felt unusually energized, needed much less sleep, talked more than usual, or felt overly confident or restless?\n\nPatient: Yes, I had a stretch a few weeks ago where I felt wired, touchy, and was talking a mile a minute. I had this sudden burst of energy where I was up before dawn reorganizing everything and felt like I was the only expert who could fix our condo community. My thoughts were racing so fast that it was incredibly hard to shut my brain off to sleep.\n\nDoctor: Have these energized periods lasted at least 4 days, and during them did you need much less sleep or do things that caused problems at work, at home, or with other people?\n\nPatient: I’m not entirely sure how many days those periods lasted or how to answer the sleep part, though I definitely had a hard time shutting my brain off. I do know that I caused some worry with my family during those times, especially when my daughter had to talk me down from a risky financial decision.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of several days or more when you felt unusually energized or irritable with much less need for sleep, more talkative, racing thoughts, or risky behavior that was clearly different from your usual self?\n\nPatient: Yes, I definitely had a stretch like that a few weeks back where I felt wired, irritable, and oddly upbeat out of nowhere, talking a mile a minute with racing thoughts. I was waking up before dawn to clean, reorganizing everything, and overcommitting to all kinds of volunteer projects. It was so unlike me that I even almost put a huge chunk of our savings into a risky housing project before my daughter talked me down.\n\nDoctor: During that energized period, did it last at least 1 week, or did it cause you to need hospitalization or seriously disrupt work, home life, or relationships?\n\nPatient: No, that energized period only lasted for several days—less than a week—and I was never hospitalized. While I almost made that big mistake with our savings, the severe disruption to my daily life and work has really been driven by the heavy, dark periods. It's the overall toll of these unpredictable swings that has made me back away from everything.\n\nDoctor: During that energized period, did it last at least 4 days in a row, and was it clearly different from your usual self?\n\nPatient: Yes, it lasted for about a week, so it was definitely at least four days, and it was completely different from my normal self. During that time, I was suddenly snapping at my husband over tiny things, talking a mile a minute, and constantly cleaning and organizing the house. It was like I was in a completely different gear and couldn't slow down."

While retiring has been a very stressful transition for me, I don't have nightmares, flashbacks, or those kinds of symptoms.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms been tied mainly to the retirement/work change and improved when the stress is less, or have they stayed present most days regardless of what is happening in your life?\n\nPatient: Even though it started with the retirement changes, these feelings haven't improved when the stress is low; they've stayed present almost every day. This heavy, hopeless depression has hung over me steadily for about a year now. Throughout this entire year, I've just kept swinging back and forth between being wound-up and sinking into these dark, heavy periods.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any period of at least two months during this past year when your mood and interest went back to normal, or has the depression been present almost continuously?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't had any breaks like that. This heavy, hopeless depression has been present almost continuously for about a year now, ever since we first started talking about my retirement. I haven't had any periods where I felt back to normal during this past year.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods during this past year when you felt unusually energized, needed much less sleep, talked more than usual, or felt overly confident or restless?\n\nPatient: Yes, I had a stretch a few weeks ago where I felt wired, touchy, and was talking a mile a minute. I had this sudden burst of energy where I was up before dawn reorganizing everything and felt like I was the only expert who could fix our condo community. My thoughts were racing so fast that it was incredibly hard to shut my brain off to sleep.\n\nDoctor: Have these energized periods lasted at least 4 days, and during them did you need much less sleep or do things that caused problems at work, at home, or with other people?\n\nPatient: I’m not entirely sure how many days those periods lasted or how to answer the sleep part, though I definitely had a hard time shutting my brain off. I do know that I caused some worry with my family during those times, especially when my daughter had to talk me down from a risky financial decision.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of several days or more when you felt unusually energized or irritable with much less need for sleep, more talkative, racing thoughts, or risky behavior that was clearly different from your usual self?\n\nPatient: Yes, I definitely had a stretch like that a few weeks back where I felt wired, irritable, and oddly upbeat out of nowhere, talking a mile a minute with racing thoughts. I was waking up before dawn to clean, reorganizing everything, and overcommitting to all kinds of volunteer projects. It was so unlike me that I even almost put a huge chunk of our savings into a risky housing project before my daughter talked me down.\n\nDoctor: During that energized period, did it last at least 1 week, or did it cause you to need hospitalization or seriously disrupt work, home life, or relationships?\n\nPatient: No, that energized period only lasted for several days—less than a week—and I was never hospitalized. While I almost made that big mistake with our savings, the severe disruption to my daily life and work has really been driven by the heavy, dark periods. It's the overall toll of these unpredictable swings that has made me back away from everything.\n\nDoctor: During that energized period, did it last at least 4 days in a row, and was it clearly different from your usual self?\n\nPatient: Yes, it lasted for about a week, so it was definitely at least four days, and it was completely different from my normal self. During that time, I was suddenly snapping at my husband over tiny things, talking a mile a minute, and constantly cleaning and organizing the house. It was like I was in a completely different gear and couldn't slow down.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period like that that lasted at least 7 days, or was severe enough to require hospitalization or cause major problems at work, home, or in relationships?"
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Those "up" periods have happened as part of my swings over the past year, usually lasting several days at a time, but I have never been hospitalized for them. They did cause problems, though, like when I almost made a reckless decision with our savings before my daughter stopped me. These swings have really disrupted my life overall, making it hard to keep up with my work and family.

(Note: In adolescents or adults, may be limited to feeling restless.)\n[S013] Quiet_Activity_Difficulty: Often unable to play or engage in leisure activities quietly.\n[S014] Constant_Activity: Is often 'on the go', acting as if 'driven by a motor'.\n[S015] Excessive_Talking: Often talks excessively.\n[S016] Blurting_Answers: Often blurts out an answer before a question has been completed.\n[S017] Waiting_Turn_Difficulty: Often has difficulty waiting his or her turn.\n[S018] Interrupting_Intruding: Often interrupts or intrudes on others (e.g., butts into conversations, games, or activities).\n[S019] Delusions: Fixed beliefs that do not change easily, even in the face of contradictory evidence. Subtypes: Derailment or Loose Associations, Tangentiality, Incoherence or Word Salad, Neologisms, Clang Associations\n[S022] Disorganized_Abnormal_Behavior: A state where behavior and motor control are severely disturbed, appearing as movements or reactions inappropriate to the situation.  Subtypes: Childlike Silliness, Stupor, Catalepsy, Waxy Flexibility, Mutism, Negativism, Posturing, Mannerism, Stereotypy, Agitation, Grimacing, Echolalia, Echopraxia\n[S023] Negative_Symptoms: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost. Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge. Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli. Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.
